## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- The champion (21626.76) uses: State 0 = Exploration (B=0), State 1 = Repulsion (B=5), State 2 = ZigZag (B=11). The repulsion behavior surprisingly scores well - it spreads robots out which may help them find the blue-walled upper strip.
- Simple 1-state repulsion (22187.85) also does well, as does GoAwayColor yellow (22680.27) and GoToColor magenta (22844.15).
- The key insight: robots need to move TOWARD BLUE walls (color 3=blue? Let me check: clr=5 is magenta, clr=4 is yellow... The color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan).
- GoAwayColor yellow (clr=4) scores 22680 - yellow walls may be the green walls perceived differently? Actually the problem says GREEN walls border the lower strip and BLUE walls border the upper strip. So GoToColor blue (clr=3) should work, and GoAwayColor green (clr=2) should also work.
- GoToColor magenta (clr=5) at 22844 is odd - perhaps the blue walls appear magenta?

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Exploit champion with small parameter tweaks
3. Explore GoToColor blue (clr=3) and GoAwayColor green (clr=2) combinations
4. Try 2-state FSMs: GoToColor + Stop on black floor
5. Try combining color-driven movement with floor detection

**Plan for 14 machines:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant: tweak probabilities slightly
3. Champion variant: change zigzag freq
4. 2-state: GoToColor blue → stop on black
5. 2-state: GoAwayColor green → stop on black
6. 2-state: GoToColor blue → zigzag
7. 3-state: explore → GoToColor blue → stop
8. 1-state: GoToColor blue (clr=3)
9. 1-state: GoAwayColor green (clr=2)
10. 2-state: repulsion → GoToColor blue on color perception
11. 3-state: GoToColor blue + floor check + stop
12. Champion variant: adjust rep gain
13. 3-state with GoAwayColor green as main driver
14. Mixed exploration

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2:**
The champion (5153.44) is dramatically better than Round 1's champion (21626.76). The winning strategy is clear:
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) - moves away from green walls (lower strip)
- State 1: Stop (B=1) - stays put when on black floor or randomly
- State 2: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) - moves toward blue walls (upper strip)

The key transitions:
- From GoAwayColor: if floor is BLACK (p=0.8) → Stop; if perceives blue (color 3, p=0.75) → GoToColor blue; if floor is GRAY (p=0.05) → GoToColor blue
- From Stop: if floor is GRAY (p=0.9) → GoAwayColor; if random (p=0.03) → GoAwayColor
- From GoToColor: if floor is BLACK (p=0.8) → Stop; if floor is GRAY (p=0.7) → GoAwayColor

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly (elitism)
2. Exploit: tweak transition probabilities in champion (higher/lower thresholds)
3. Exploit: try swapping state order or adding a 4th state
4. Exploit: use champion #2 (GoToColor blue + stop) with minor tweaks

6. Explore: different stop conditions (neighbor-based)

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**
The champion improved to 4781.61. The winning structure is very consistent:
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) - flees green walls
- State 1: Stop (B=1) - stays put on black floor
- State 2: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) - moves toward blue walls

Key transitions in champion:
- S0→S1 on black floor (p=0.8), S0→S1 on perceive blue (p=0.75), S0→S1 on gray (p=0.05)
- S1→S0 on gray (p=0.9), S1→S0 random (p=0.03), S1→S2 on perceive blue (p=0.4)
- S2→S1 on black (p=0.8), S2→S0 on gray (p=0.7), S2→S0 on perceive green (p=0.5)

The key insight: having Stop state transition to GoToColor blue when seeing blue (l1x2=3, p=0.4) is important. This lets robots already in the upper strip stay, while others actively seek blue.

4-state versions performed WORST - avoid those.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly (elitism)
2. Tweak champion probabilities: the p=0.4 blue-perception transition from Stop seems crucial - try 0.3, 0.5, 0.6
3. Try adjusting the stop→GoAwayColor gray probability (0.9)
4. Try adding a neighbor-count condition to the stop state to prevent leaving when surrounded
5. Try the champion structure with slightly different color perception thresholds
6. Explore: pure 2-state versions closer to champion topology

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**
The champion (4406.40) uses p0x0=0.82, p0x1=0.77 (slightly higher than 0.8/0.75), and p2x2=0.52 (slightly higher than 0.5). The second-best adds a 4th transition from S1 that goes back to S0 on seeing green (l1x3=2, p=0.35) - this is interesting as it prevents getting stuck.

Key observations:
- p=0.45 for S1→S2 is WORSE than p=0.4 (in worst performers)
- p=0.35 for S1→S2 is better than p=0.4 in some cases (4468 vs champion)
- Adding S1→S0 on seeing green (p=0.35) gives 4425 (second best)
- p=0.92 for S1→S0 on gray is WORSE than p=0.9
- p1x1 (gray exit from stop) = 0.9 is consistently good

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Fine-tune around p0x0=0.82, p0x1=0.77, p2x2=0.52
- Try the 4-transition S1 variant with various green-perception probs
- Try p=0.30, 0.32, 0.38 for S1→S2 blue perception
- Try p2x2=0.5, 0.52, 0.55 for green perception in S2

**Exploration:**
- Try combining the best S1 (4-transition) with the best S2 parameters from champion
- Try adding a 4th transition to S0 (see blue directly → go to S2)

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**
The champion remains at 4406.40 - no improvement for 3 rounds. The search has converged tightly around this topology.

- p0x0=0.82, p0x1=0.77 seem optimal (0.83/0.78 is WORSE, 0.8/0.75 is slightly worse)

- The 4-transition S1 variants (p1x3=0.3 or 0.35 on seeing green) score ~4595, close but not better
- p1x1=0.025 instead of 0.03 gives ~4602

The local optimum seems strong. I need more aggressive exploration now:
1. Try completely different S0 behavior - maybe Exploration instead of GoAwayColor

4. Try adding a neighbor-based condition to prevent stop-state departure when surrounded
5. Try different color perception thresholds more aggressively (p=0.2, 0.6, 0.7 for blue perception from stop)
6. Try a 2-state machine with just GoToBlue + Stop but with the champion's parameter insights

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**
Major breakthrough! The champion improved to 4201.07. The key change: S2 (GoToColor blue) now has `--c2x1 0` (black floor) instead of `--c2x1 1` (gray floor) for the second transition, with p=0.5. This means:
- S2→S1 on black floor (p=0.9) - primary stop trigger
- S2→S0 on BLACK floor (p=0.5) - secondary, sends to GoAwayGreen on black! 

Wait, that's interesting - two black floor transitions from S2. The second one (p=0.5) goes to S0 (GoAwayColor green). This creates a more dynamic behavior where robots on the black floor sometimes go back to GoAwayGreen, potentially correcting wrong-strip robots.

**Key findings:**
- S2 second transition changed from gray (c=1) to black (c=0) with p=0.5 → big improvement
- p1x2=0.2 (stop→GoToBlue on blue-seen) gives 4459 - worse than 0.4
- p2x2=0.35 gives 4480 - worth exploring further

**Exploitation:**
- Try different p values for the new black-floor transition from S2 (0.4, 0.45, 0.55, 0.6)
- Try different p2x0 (0.85, 0.88, 0.92, 0.95) for black floor in S2
- Try adding a 4th transition to S2

**Exploration:**
- Try making both S2 transitions black-floor with different probabilities
- Try the same black-floor trick in S0 (two black-floor transitions)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.82 m. 23 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.78 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.51 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
